- record_id: BRD-077
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "Six consecutive loads of an 8 MiB cartridge all boot correctly: the bimodality of BRD-014 and BRD-045 does not reproduce"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "The same image, `build/test/cv_aos.gba` (Castlevania Aria of Sorrow, 8,388,608 bytes), was loaded over UART six times in succession on one `build/snd_prod` bitstream with no power cycle, reset or rebuild between loads, and captured 14 seconds after each load. All six booted to the title sequence and all six were captured mid-animation on the eclipse logo screen, with the moon ring, the starburst and the castle visible in every frame. The six captures carry four distinct hashes, with three of them byte-identical: the title screen animates, so identical hashes mean the animation was caught at the same phase, which shows the boot timing is repeatable and not merely that six attempts happened to succeed. The test exists because BRD-014 recorded this machine as bimodal across loads of one fixed bitstream -- one load halting in the first second and another running for twenty -- and BRD-045 recorded the same cartridge, Super Mario Advance, freezing in `REQ1_WAIT` on one load and running on another. Both records concluded that any future reproduction had to take several loads and say which behaviour was seen, because a single capture was a coin flip."
  consequence: "The operator watched and listened to each of the six boots individually and reported that all six looked and sounded perfect, which adds a second observer to the captures and extends the result past what the frames can show: the captures establish that the picture was correct at the sampled moment, and the ear establishes that the boot music and the audio path were correct on every load as well. That matters because an audio defect would not necessarily change the frame, so a run of six identical pictures could in principle hide six differently-broken sound paths; the operator report rules that out for these six.\n\nThis is the first time the project has taken six loads of one image and got the same answer every time, and it is evidence that the bimodality was a symptom of the defects since fixed rather than a permanent property of the machine. The chain is consistent: the bimodality was recorded on bitstreams that carried the entry-14 `REQ2_START` early exit, which made a double request's completion depend on where the SDRAM controller's acknowledgement slot fell relative to the `clk16` edge, and that in turn depended on the phase between two PLLs which could lock differently per load; with the lines returned to upstream the completion no longer depends on that phase, so there is nothing left to vary. That explanation is an inference and not a measurement -- the phase relationship was never instrumented -- so what is established here is only that six loads agreed, which is a much stronger statement than one load and still not a proof of determinism. Six is also a small sample for a fault that was previously described as a coin flip: if the true failure probability were one in ten, six clean loads would happen about half the time. A larger sample would be needed to claim the fault is gone rather than merely rare, and no claim beyond six is made."
  sources:
    - "Six loads of `build/test/cv_aos.gba`, each `sent 8388608 image bytes in 42.7s`, captured at `/tmp/sixloads/L1.png` through `L6.png`"
    - "Hashes of the active area: `7616212870`, `890fe0480a`, `de6a567073`, `890fe0480a`, `890fe0480a`, `88e4f93c4f` -- four distinct, three identical"
    - "Bitstream `build/snd_prod/tinygba.fs` from commit `8543346`, unchanged across all six loads"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: worked perfect on my end, and separately that all six boots looked and sounded perfect when watched and listened to individually"
    - "Records BRD-014, BRD-045, BRD-059, BRD-066 and BRD-075"

- record_id: TCL-054
  kind: TOOLCHAIN
  topic_id: TCL
  title: "A .fs flashed with openFPGALoader runs the core but configures no controller; the same core loaded with tangload does"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "The same TinyNES release image, byte-identical either way, behaves differently depending on how it reaches the FPGA. Flashed with `openFPGALoader -b tangconsole <tinynes.fs>` in one-wire mode, the core boots, runs Zelda with correct video and audio, and reports `PAD STATUS 50000000 ... LINES=ff` -- low byte 0x00, so `present=0` and `classic=0` on all four buses, with about 80 failed I2C transactions and zero reads, and the player prints `PAD1=absent PAD2=absent`. Loaded through TinyTang with `tangload /root/cores/console138k/tinynes.bin` in two-wire mode, the same core on the same board reports `PAD1 connected classic ID=010320a40000 FAIL=0` on the first poll, with status `0039f603`. Neither the image, the adapter, the controller nor the core differs between the two runs; only the loading path does. The mechanism is in TinyTang: `tang_ini_core_loaded()` in `ports/bl616/phosphor/pmod_sockets.cpp` sets the I2C-bus belief for the loaded core and then calls `send(*core)` with the declaration parsed from `/tinytang.ini`, so the socket layout reaches the core as part of the load. A bare JTAG flash sends nothing, so the core starts with no socket configuration and its pad buses stay dark. A second, independent factor is present too: in one-wire mode the BL616 runs the vendor's FT2232 USB Debugger and TinyTang does not run at all, which is BRD-006 of the TinyNES record set, so there is no `tangload` and no console to send the declaration from."
  consequence: "Every TinyGBA hardware result before this record was taken on an image flashed with `openFPGALoader` and is therefore valid for video, audio, memory, CPU and cartridge behaviour, but says nothing about controller input: under that path no controller could have worked, and the absence was a property of the deployment rather than of the core. It also means a controller cannot be added to TinyGBA by porting `wii_pads.sv` alone. The core would have to be loaded through TinyTang and to accept the socket declaration on the same terms TinyNES does, which is a larger change than an RTL port and should be planned as such. The practical rule for this project: when a result depends on anything on the USB-A or PMOD connectors, state the loading path with the result, because the same bitstream answers differently under the two. This cost most of an hour of wrong hypotheses -- an unpowered adapter, then an I2C mux channel -- both of which the measurement refuted."
  sources:
    - "One-wire: `openFPGALoader -b tangconsole build/release_0.1.0/tinynes.fs`, then `load_ae350.py pad_test.elf` on `/dev/ttyUSB1`: `PAD STATUS 50000000 ... LINES=ff`, `PAD1=absent`"
    - "Two-wire: `tangload /root/cores/console138k/tinynes.bin` on the TinyTang shell at `/dev/ttyACM1`, then `load_ae350.py --tinytang --port /dev/ttyACM1 pad_test.elf`: `PAD1 connected classic ID=010320a40000 FAIL=0`"
    - "Same `.fs` both ways: SHA-256 `2a356060084bad434a19...`, byte-identical to the TinyTang v0.2.1 archive entry for `tinynes.bin` at `5cd4ab004f7af3c9...`"
    - "This repository's vendored TinyTang reference and `ports/bl616/phosphor/pmod_sockets.cpp` `tang_ini_core_loaded()`"
    - "TinyNES `.ai/core-reference.md` BRD-006, on which firmware the BL616 runs under which power input"

- record_id: BRD-078
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "TinyGBA ships as a tinygba.bin/tinygba.fs.gz pair on the TinyTang card and runs in two-wire mode, but receives no controller input"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "TinyGBA now exists as a first-class core on the TinyTang card, alongside tiny89, tinygbc, tinygba, tinynes, tinysnes, tinyphosphor and tinytang. The pair was produced from `build/snd_prod` -- the image that runs Castlevania Aria of Sorrow -- by copying the `.bin` the build already emits and gzipping the `.fs`, giving `tinygba.bin` 5,000,172 bytes (sha256 `f047a77ea0f1d854...`) and `tinygba.fs.gz` 1,404,785 bytes (sha256 `acb4185eecf69bb4...`), matching the sizes and forms of the shipped cores: TinyNES ships 4,777,964 and 795,315 for the same pair. Both were written to `/root/cores/console138k/` over the USB CDC console with `tools/tinytang_put.py` in 6.9 and 4.1 seconds and confirmed with `ls -l` from the board's own shell. `tangload /root/cores/console138k/tinygba.bin` programmed the FPGA and reported `tangload: core loaded`; the RetroRegen diagnostic cartridge was then streamed through `fpgapipe` and drew its title screen, 503,407 lit pixels, the same figure as the one-wire run. So the core loads, boots and runs under two-wire. Input does not: with `tools/roms/keylive.s` running, `KEYINPUT` reads `0x3FF` with nothing held and still `0x3FF` while a button on the qualified Classic Controller is held down, which is the correct and expected result because TinyGBA has no Wii-extension I2C logic at all -- `wii_pads.sv` exists only in TinyNES, and TinyGBA's keypad is fed only from `controller_ds2` and two `usb_hid_host` instances whose `USB1` and `USB2` defines are commented out in `console138k.v`, leaving `joy_usb1` and `joy_usb2` tied to zero."
  consequence: "Two-wire deployment is now established for this project and is the only path on which a controller can ever work, per TCL-054. That makes the controller a well-scoped next project rather than an unknown: the port needs `wii_pads.sv` plus its I2C pin constraints, a register block to expose the pad state, and -- the part that is easy to miss -- the core must be loaded through TinyTang and accept the socket declaration for the pad fabric to be configured at all. Until that exists, every GBA result in this project is a result about the machine with a host-supplied controller, not with the board's own. Nothing else regressed: the same bitstream that runs a retail title under one-wire runs identically under two-wire, and the pairing on the card is the same image that produced BRD-075."
  sources:
    - "`build/ship/tinygba.bin` 5,000,172 bytes sha256 `f047a77ea0f1d854...`, `build/ship/tinygba.fs.gz` 1,404,785 bytes sha256 `acb4185eecf69bb4...`"
    - "`tools/tinytang_put.py --port /dev/ttyACM1` transferring both to `/root/cores/console138k/`"
    - "TinyTang shell at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `ls -l /root/cores/console138k/` lists tinygba.bin and tinygba.fs.gz with the other cores"
    - "`tangload /root/cores/console138k/tinygba.bin` -> `tangload: core loaded`"
    - "Board capture `/tmp/gba2w_rom.png`: diag cart title screen, 503,407 lit pixels"
    - "Board capture `/tmp/kl_held.png`: keylive all red, bar white, `KEYINPUT` 0x3FF while a Classic Controller button was held"
    - "Records BRD-075, TCL-054, and TinyNES's `wii_pads.sv`"

- record_id: TCL-055
  kind: TOOLCHAIN
  topic_id: TCL
  title: "`gzip` stamps the wall clock into its header, so a plain `gzip -9` of one bitstream gives a different hash every run"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "The TinyGBA card pairing was first produced by copying the build's `.bin` and running `gzip -9` on the `.fs` by hand, which put `tinygba.fs.gz` at 1,404,785 bytes with SHA-256 `acb4185eecf69bb4...`. Rebuilding the same bitstream with `tools/ship_core.py`, which sets `mtime=0` in the `GzipFile` header, gives 1,401,896 bytes with SHA-256 `5a8ad985b2687bac...` from the same input, and running that tool twice in a row gives byte-identical output both times. The input did not change between the two: `tinygba.bin` is `f047a77ea0f1d854...` in every case. The whole difference is the four-byte MTIME field gzip writes from the current time unless it is told otherwise, plus deflate's response to it. `.ai/core.md` requires a generated artifact the operator uses to come from a committed deterministic script, and a `gzip` invocation that is not told `mtime=0` cannot satisfy that, so the hand-run command was not merely untidy but non-conforming."
  consequence: "Any artifact this project records by hash must be produced with the clock excluded, or the hash identifies the run rather than the content and cannot be compared between two trees or two agents. The practical consequence for the card specifically: a `.fs.gz` whose hash is not reproducible cannot be used to tell whether the card holds the image that was built, which is exactly the question a handoff needs answered. `tools/ship_core.py` now produces both halves of the pair, copies the build's own `.bin` rather than repacking it so there is one converter in the tree, refuses a missing or empty `.bin`, and prints both hashes for the record. The `.bin` on the card was unaffected throughout -- `tangload` reads that file, not the `.gz` -- so the two-wire result in BRD-078 stands for the image that was measured."
  sources:
    - "`gzip -9` by hand: `tinygba.fs.gz` 1,404,785 bytes, SHA-256 `acb4185eecf69bb4...`"
    - "`tools/ship_core.py build/snd_prod`: `tinygba.fs.gz` 1,401,896 bytes, SHA-256 `5a8ad985b2687bac...`, identical across two consecutive runs"
    - "`tinygba.bin` SHA-256 `f047a77ea0f1d854...` unchanged"
    - "This repository, `tools/ship_core.py`"

- record_id: TCL-056
  kind: TOOLCHAIN
  topic_id: TCL
  title: "The two-flip-flop audio fix costs 3.4x on clk67 by moving ABC's optimisation boundary, and no placement seed recovers it"
  status: MEASURED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "BRD-074 recorded that `build/snd_prod` needed the documented GBATang multicycle relaxation at `clk67` 53.622 MHz where `build/rev_prod` reached 181.061 MHz without one, and attributed the difference to 'placement variance from one seed ... a seed sweep would recover it'. A 24-seed sweep of the current tree, with a matched control, shows the attribution is wrong and the cost is the audio fix itself. Ten placements of the current netlist at seeds 2, 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12 and 13 give `clk67` between 48.633 and 57.172 MHz with a mean of 53.75, and every one of them passes only through the documented relaxation; the distribution does not overlap the control and has no clean seed in it. Two placements of the `rev_prod` netlist at the same seeds give 189.970 MHz at seed 4 and 184.060 MHz at seed 10, neither needing a relaxation, so at a fixed seed the two netlists differ by about 130 MHz. The netlists differ by six cells and two flip-flops: 49,562 cells and 8,534 `DFFRE` for `rev_prod` against 49,556 and 8,536 for the current tree, and `git diff f38cebe..HEAD -- fpga/gba/src/` touches only `gba_sound.v`, `gba_sound_ch1.v`, `gba_sound_ch3.v` and `gba_sound_ch4.v`. The cause was isolated directly: the current tree was synthesised with only those four files returned to their `f38cebe` state, which produced a netlist identical to `rev_prod` at 49,562 cells and 8,534 `DFFRE`, and placed at seed 10 that netlist gave 184.060 MHz with no relaxation -- the same figure as the `rev_prod` control at the same seed, to the digit. The mechanism is a change of worst path rather than a longer one: `rev_prod`'s worst `clk16`-to-`clk67` path is `cpu.cmd_DFFRE_Q_9` to `sdram.f_mode_DFFRE_Q_1` at 13.94 ns over 29 segments, while the current tree's is `mem.eeprom.m_eeprom.dpb_inst_0` to `sdram.f_mode_DFFRE_Q` at 39.96 ns over 63 segments, so adding two flip-flops in the sound block moved ABC's optimisation boundary and rerouted the cone that feeds the cartridge-flash mode register in `sdram_gba.v`."
  consequence: "Two things follow and they point in opposite directions. First, BRD-074's placement-variance sentence is refuted and must not be quoted: the margin is a property of the netlist, no seed recovers it, and a future agent that spends a sweep looking for one is repeating this cycle. Second, the cost is not a defect in the audio fix, which BRD-073 and BRD-074 verify on the board at 439.4 Hz and 3331.7 RMS; it is the open flow's sensitivity to small changes, which entries 6 and 20 already record as ABC choosing a different optimisation boundary. The build is not broken: `build/hyg_s1` passed all nine gates, including the two added this cycle, and packed a bitstream, and the documented relaxation it needs is the one upstream GBATang's own `gbatang.sdc` exists to express. What is now worth doing, and is a separate change needing its own approval, is to shorten the `sdram.f_mode` cone structurally rather than hope for a seed -- registering the address that feeds `mem_bios` or the flash-mode decode one level before the port is the shape TCL-035 already names for a different array. Until that is done, any image built from a tree carrying the audio fix needs the documented relaxation and must say so when its timing is quoted."
  sources:
    - "This repository, `build/hyg_sweep/s2,4,5,6,8,9,10,11,12,13/timing-gate.log` -- ten current-tree placements, `clk67` 48.633 to 57.172 MHz, each with exactly one ALLOWED line"
    - "This repository, `build/revsweep/s4/` and `build/revsweep/s10/` -- the `rev_prod` netlist at the same seeds, 189.970 and 184.060 MHz, no relaxation"
    - "This repository, `build/iso_snd/` -- the current tree with `fpga/gba/src/sound/` returned to `f38cebe`, netlist 49,562 cells and 8,534 `DFFRE`, seed 10, 184.060 MHz, no relaxation"
    - "This repository, `build/hyg_s1/timing-gate.log` (53.622-class result at seed 1) and `build/rev_prod/timing-gate.log` (181.061 MHz, no relaxation)"
    - "`git diff f38cebe..HEAD -- fpga/gba/src/` touching only the four sound files, 102 insertions and 20 deletions"
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/memory/sdram_gba.v`: `reg [2:0] f_mode`, the cartridge-flash mode register the worst path terminates at"
    - "Records BRD-072, BRD-073, BRD-074, TCL-035, TCL-036"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 by three independent comparisons, all read from `tools/timing_gate.py` on routed netlists and not from nextpnr's own verdict. A ten-seed sweep of the current netlist; a two-seed control of the `rev_prod` netlist at two of the same seeds; and a causal build in which only the four audio files were reverted, whose netlist reproduced `rev_prod`'s cell and flip-flop counts exactly and whose placement at seed 10 reproduced the control's 184.060 MHz to the digit. Source state was confirmed with `git diff --stat` before and after the causal build, and the tree was restored with `git checkout HEAD -- fpga/gba/src/sound/` with the four files verified byte-identical to `HEAD` afterwards. Not confirmed on hardware: no bitstream from this cycle was flashed, and whether the 39.96 ns path behaves on silicon as the timing gate predicts has not been measured."

- record_id: BRD-079
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "The board's own controller works: a Wii Classic Controller on USB-A port 2 drives the GBA keypad with the intended mapping"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "TinyGBA now reads a Wii Classic Controller directly, with no host in the loop. `fpga/gba/src/peripherals/wii_pad_master.sv` -- TinyNES's `wii_pad_master`, vendored byte-identical -- is instantiated in `gbatang_top.sv` on USB-A port 2's data pair with D+ (`usb1_dp`, H13) as SDA and D- (`usb1_dn`, G13) as SCL through two open-drain `IOBUF`s, clocked by `clk50` at a 126-quarter bit time (50.251 / (4 x 126) = 99.7 kHz), and its report is synchronised into `clk16` through two flops before being folded into `joy_btns_gba`. `build/pad_s14` (seed 14, `TINYGBA_OVERLAY_OFF`, official BIOS, `-nowidelut` by default, `.fs` SHA-256 `888cfdd44e3cbd41...`) passed every gate -- zero conflicting drivers, register parity +194 flops (1.7%), memory census unchanged, no new deep `ADB` cone, clock edges OK, hold 0, `clk67` on `SPINE25/TOP1` and `hclk` across `SPINE26/BOTTOM2`, both measured good per BRD-048 -- and was written to the card and loaded with `tangload` in two-wire mode, which is the only path on which a controller can work at all (TCL-054). With `build/test/keylive.gba` running, the operator pressed the controller and reported that **A and X both light the leftmost square (GBA A), B and Y both light the second square (GBA B), and every other button lights its own square**. That is exactly the intended mapping: the D-pad, A, B, START, SELECT, L and R one-to-one, X aliased onto A and Y onto B to preserve the Classic Controller's physical face-button arrangement, ZL and ZR aliased onto L and R, and HOME left unmapped. Both IOBUFs placed at `X181Y97/IOBA` and `X181Y97/IOBB` with `hdlname usb1_dp`/`usb1_dn`, confirmed in the routed netlist before the load rather than inferred, and the 375-cell pad instance (145 flops) traced forward from `joy_wii` into `joypad.Keys_DFF_Q_4_D_LUT4_F`, the keypad register's data input."
  consequence: "This closes the last missing input capability. Before it, every GBA result in this project was a result about the machine with a host-supplied controller: `tools/gba_input.py` could drive a menu over the core's UART (BRD-068), but nothing on the board itself could press a button, and BRD-078 measured `KEYINPUT` reading 0x3FF while a qualified controller's button was held. The board is now a complete machine for play, not only for measurement. Two limits are deliberately stated and should travel with this record. The analogue sticks and both analogue triggers are read by the master and discarded, because the GBA has no analogue input, so LEFT and RIGHT come from the D-pad only. And the report distinguishes A/X and B/Y but does not separately confirm ZL and ZR: those share the same OR terms as L and R, so they cannot differ from them except in which pad bit is set, and the operator's report of every other button lighting its own square is consistent with them working without being a separate observation of each. Nothing else regressed: the same image's gates were clean and the cartridge path is untouched by this change."
  sources:
    - "This repository, `build/pad_s14/` -- `build.log`, `timing-gate.log`, `clock-plane.log`, `parity.log`, `memory-census.log`, `bsram-addr.log`; `.fs` SHA-256 `888cfdd44e3cbd41204ace6d97761fc1aee05509fe912a7f7f6ee64ac58c4b5a`"
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/gbatang_top.sv`: the `pad0` instantiation, both `IOBUF`s, the `pad_held` active-low inversion, the two-flop `clk16` synchroniser and the `joy_wii` mapping"
    - "This repository, `build/pad_s14/tinygba-routed.json`: both `IOBUF`s at `X181Y97` with `hdlname usb1_dp` and `usb1_dn`"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14, pressing the controller against `keylive.gba`"
    - "TinyNES `README.md` (USB-A port 2 wiring, adapter pull-up division) and `firmware/apps/pad_test/main.c` (the button bit table), at commit `7013393`"
    - "Records BRD-078, TCL-054, TCL-057"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 on the board: `build/pad_s14` written to the card with `tools/tinytang_put.py`, loaded with `tangload` in two-wire mode reporting `tangload: core loaded`, `build/test/keylive.gba` streamed through `fpgapipe`, and the operator pressing the controller and reporting A and X on the leftmost square, B and Y on the second, and every other button on its own square. The bit positions were confirmed in the routed netlist before the load -- both `IOBUF`s at `X181Y97` with `hdlname usb1_dp`/`usb1_dn`, and `joy_wii` tracing into `joypad.Keys_DFF_Q_4_D_LUT4_F` -- rather than inferred from the RTL. Independently of the board, `tools/pad/run_pad_tb.sh` passes for both a genuine Classic Controller and an 8BitDo receiver against a byte-level model of the part, with zero I2C NACKs. Not measured: the analogue sticks and triggers, which are discarded, and ZL and ZR as separate observations -- they share OR terms with L and R, so the operator seeing every other button on its own square is consistent with them working without being a distinct test of each."

- record_id: TCL-057
  kind: TOOLCHAIN
  topic_id: TCL
  title: "A vendored module stays diffable only if its body is byte-identical, and a button table must come from validated firmware rather than a testbench comment"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "Two rules were applied to bring TinyNES's Wii pad master into this tree and both paid immediately. First, `fpga/gba/src/peripherals/wii_pad_master.sv` was produced by extracting the module verbatim from TinyNES `fpga/ae350_soc/wii_pads.sv` and prepending only a provenance header, so `tail -n +13` of the result diffs clean against the upstream module -- which is what makes it possible to tell an upstream defect from a local edit later, and is the shape `THIRD_PARTY.md` records. Only that one module was taken: upstream's file also carries `wii_pads`, an AHB slave with a four-bus register file for the AE350 SoC, and TinyGBA has no AHB bus, so taking it would have meant porting a bus and a memory map to reach one controller's buttons. The same treatment was applied to the controller model, `tools/pad/wii_classic_model.sv`, extracted from TinyNES `tests/wii_pads_tb.sv`. Second, the button bit positions were taken from `firmware/apps/pad_test/main.c`, the table the operator validated on this board against a genuine Classic Controller and an 8BitDo Retro Receiver, and not from the comment in TinyNES's own testbench, which labels its sample report 'Up held' while the validated table maps that same bit (pad bit 10) to ZR. Hardware settles the disagreement in favour of the firmware table: on this board every button lights its own square under the table's assignment. The regression test is `tools/pad/run_pad_tb.sh`, which drives the vendored master against the byte-level controller model through the real open-drain wiring, synchroniser and mapping, and checks both the genuine-Classic and receiver paths. It reports `present=1 classic=1 reads=1 fails=0`, with `data` matching TinyNES's recorded hardware words (`0x62101fa0` genuine, `0x001021a1` receiver), and asserts that the model's one held button lands on GBA R. It needs iverilog only and runs in seconds because `US_CLOCKS` is compressed; everything it checks is independent of that compression."
  consequence: "The test was mutation-tested before being trusted: deleting the ZR alias from the R term makes it fail with `joy_wii=000, expected only R (bit 11)`. Two things follow. A vendored file that has been edited in place cannot be diffed against its source, so the discipline is to keep the body untouched and put every adaptation in the consumer -- here, in `gbatang_top.sv` -- which is also why the mapping lives there rather than in the vendored module. And when a project vendors two artefacts from the same upstream (a module and its model), their own documentation may disagree with the firmware that was validated on hardware; the firmware table is the authority because it was checked against real buttons, and a testbench comment is not. The wider point is that this class of integration -- a foreign protocol master plus a mapping table -- is exactly where a wrong bit is invisible: it produces a working controller that presses the wrong button, which a user reports as one button misbehaving and which reads as a wiring fault. Checking it in simulation against a known-good model costs seconds; finding it on the board costs a build, a flash and an operator session, and would have been misdiagnosed as hardware."
  sources:
    - "TinyNES `fpga/ae350_soc/wii_pads.sv` module `wii_pad_master` and `tests/wii_pads_tb.sv` module `wii_classic_model`, both at `7013393`; `tail -n +13 tools/pad/wii_classic_model.sv | diff - <upstream>` is empty"
    - "TinyNES `firmware/apps/pad_test/main.c` button table: bit 15 ZL, 14 B, 13 Y, 12 A, 11 X, 10 ZR, 9 LEFT, 8 UP, 7 RIGHT, 6 DOWN, 5 L, 4 SELECT, 3 HOME, 2 START, 1 R"
    - "This repository, `tools/pad/tb_pad.sv`, `tools/pad/wii_classic_model.sv` and `tools/pad/run_pad_tb.sh`; both paths PASS, mutation of the R term FAILS"
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/gbatang_top.sv`: the mapping, which is deliberately in the consumer and not in the vendored file"
    - "Records BRD-079 and BRD-078"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14: `tools/pad/run_pad_tb.sh` exits 0 with both the classic and receiver paths reporting `present=1 classic=1 reads=1 fails=0`, `data=fbff62101fa0` and `data=fbff001021a1` respectively, and `joy_wii=800`; the mutation run exits 1. The bit named in the operator's hardware report and the bit the mapping produces agree, which is what raises the button table from plausible to confirmed on this board."

- record_id: TCL-058
  kind: TOOLCHAIN
  topic_id: TCL
  title: "The core runs at 99.8378% of a real GBA's frame rate because the PLL cannot make 16.777216 MHz from a 27 MHz reference, and it is inaudible"
  status: MEASURED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "A GBA's frame rate is a hardware constant and not a property of any cartridge: the CPU clock is 16.777216 MHz (2^24 Hz) and a frame is 280,896 cycles (308 scanlines x 912 cycles), so every GBA runs at 59.7275 frames per second. This core does not, and the reason is entirely in the clock plan. `fpga/gba/src/pll/pll_16.v` sets `FCLKIN(\"27\")`, `MDIV_SEL(67)`, `MDIV_FRAC_SEL(0)` and `ODIV2_SEL(108)`, so FVCO is 27 x 67 = 1809 MHz and `clk16` is 1809 / 108 = 16.750000 MHz exactly -- against the 16.777216 MHz a GBA uses. The frame rate follows directly: 16.750000e6 / 280896 = 59.6306 Hz against 59.7275 Hz, a ratio of 99.8378 percent, or an error of -0.1622 percent. The figure was confirmed by measurement as well as arithmetic: a 30-second capture of The Legend of Zelda: The Minish Cap through the capture card at 60 fps yielded 1800 frames of which 14 consecutive pairs were near-identical under a tolerance comparison, an implied output of about 59.5 to 59.6 fps, which matches the PLL prediction and not 60. The first attempt at that count hashed frames exactly and reported 1799 of 1800 distinct, because MJPEG quantisation noise makes even a duplicate frame hash differently; the tolerance comparison is the one that works and exact hashing must not be used to count frames in a capture."
  consequence: "The deficit is a clock-plan compromise rather than a speed limit: the netlist has ample timing margin and loses no frames. 1809 MHz is chosen because it divides evenly to every clock this design needs -- 1809/36 = 50.25 MHz for `clk50`, 1809/27 = 67 MHz for `clk67`, 1809/108 = 16.75 MHz for `clk16`, and the SDRAM pin clock from the same VCO -- and no exact 16.777216 MHz is reachable from a 27 MHz reference without a fractional divider. 16.777216 MHz would need FVCO 1812.0 MHz, which from 27 MHz is `MDIV` 67.11 and would require `MDIV_FRAC_SEL`; that VCO feeds every clock in the design, so taking it would mean re-verifying the entire clock plan and the SDRAM controller's timing for a 0.16 percent gain. It is not worth doing. What the error does produce is audible only as slow drift against a reference running at exactly real speed: at 0.162 percent the beat period between two tone generators is 1.4 s at 440 Hz but 5 to 10 s at 60 to 120 Hz, which is exactly the slow resynchronisation the operator heard when comparing this core against mGBA on The Minish Cap's bass line -- and mGBA is the one running at true speed, not the core that is lagging. State the figure as 99.84 percent whenever core speed is described, and do not describe the core as running at full speed or as being limited by performance."
  sources:
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/pll/pll_16.v`: `FCLKIN(\"27\")`, `MDIV_SEL(67)`, `MDIV_FRAC_SEL(0)`, `ODIV2_SEL(108)`, `ODIV0_SEL(36)`, `ODIV1_SEL(27)`"
    - "The GBA's CPU clock 16.777216 MHz and 280,896 cycles per frame: the AGB hardware timing, 1232 cycles per scanline x 228 lines of which 160 are visible and the rest vertical blank, giving 308 total lines x 912 cycles"
    - "This repository, capture `/tmp/fps/cap.mp4`, 30 s at 60 fps through `/dev/video0`, 1800 frames, 14 near-identical consecutive pairs by mean-absolute-difference"
    - "Record BRD-080 for the gameplay result this was measured against"
  verification: "Computed 2026-11-14 from the PLL parameters and the AGB timing constant, and cross-checked by a frame count on a 30-second capture. The arithmetic is exact given the PLL settings (1809/108 = 16.75 MHz, no fractional divider involved). Not measured with a frequency counter on the board, which would settle the last digit; the two independent methods agree to within the resolution of the capture method."

- record_id: BRD-080
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "The Minish Cap and Metroid Fusion run correctly on the board, but Pokemon Emerald stops at the BIOS handover's white screen"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "Three cartridges were loaded on the board from the card with `gbaload` and compared against mGBA 0.10.5 running the same ROM with the same retail BIOS. Metroid Fusion (USA), 8,388,608 bytes, boots through the BIOS intro to the `Nintendo Presents` screen and its Samus Data file select, and the operator passed it for both audio and video. The Legend of Zelda: The Minish Cap (USA), 16,777,216 bytes, draws its title screen -- the Zelda logo, the Minish Cap artwork with Ezlo, the forest background, `PRESS START` and the `(C)2004,2005 Nintendo` line -- with 144k to 163k distinct colours across the sampled frames and every frame unique, and mGBA reached the same game's `CHOOSE A FILE` screen. The operator passed Minish Cap for audio and video and, comparing it against mGBA on the same title, reported that the audio starts in sync, drifts and resynchronises on a period of five to ten seconds against the bass line; TCL-058 shows that is the core's 0.1622 percent clock deficit beating against a reference at true speed and is not a fault. Pokemon Emerald Version (USA, Europe), 16,777,216 bytes, does not run: it boots the BIOS and then stops on the uniform white field the BIOS leaves at handover, with `DISPCNT` still in forced blank, on two separate loads. Emerald was loaded from the card after a fresh `tangload` of the core in two-wire mode, so the load itself succeeded -- the loader reported `16777216 bytes in; the core is running` -- and the failure is after handover."
  consequence: "Two of three cartridges run and one does not, and the one that fails does so in the same way BRD-060 recorded before the memory and DMA defects were fixed, which means that failure shape has more than one cause and must not be attributed to the old defects. The operator's hypothesis is worth recording because the RTL supports it in one half and not the other. Save hardware: `gba_memory.sv`'s `isreadonly` returns 1 for regions `4'hE` and `4'hF` unless `config_backup_type` is `BACKUP_FLASH512K`, `BACKUP_FLASH1M` or `BACKUP_SRAM` (lines 937-941), and since `tools/load_gba_rom.py` -- and now `gbaload` -- never enter the loader's backup-type stage, `config_backup_type` stays `BACKUP_NONE` and every write the cartridge makes to its save window is discarded. Reads are not blocked: `tosdram` maps regions `4'hE` and `4'hF` to a 64 KB cartridge-RAM window in SDRAM (`4'b111?: tosdram = {10'b1000_0001_00, addr[15:2]}`), so a cartridge sees a readable save area that never changes. RTC and GPIO are worse than misconfigured: `gba_memory.sv` and `gbatang_top.sv` contain no cartridge RTC or GPIO implementation at all, so reads of the RTC registers Emerald uses return whatever the bus decodes to rather than a plausible clock. Emerald reads both the save and the RTC early, which is consistent with a stop at handover, but this is a lead and not a finding: no instrument here has measured which access blocks, and the next test is to add the backup-type stage to `gbaload` and see whether Emerald advances. What this record does establish is that Metroid Fusion and The Minish Cap are sound on the board across audio and video, and that Emerald is the first retail title found to fail since BRD-066 fixed the machine."
  sources:
    - "This repository, captures of Metroid Fusion (`/tmp/padcap/rl/`, `/tmp/padcap/mf2/`) and The Minish Cap (`/tmp/padcap/mcf/`) through `/dev/video0` at 1920x1080"
    - "`gbaload: 8388608 bytes in; the core is running` and `gbaload: 16777216 bytes in; the core is running` from the board's console at `/dev/ttyACM1`"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: Metroid Fusion pass, Minish Cap pass with the 5-10 s audio resync, Pokemon Emerald boot to a white screen and stop, twice"
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/memory/gba_memory.sv` lines 937-941 (`isreadonly`), the `4'b111?:` arm of `tosdram`, and line 760 where `config_backup_type <= BACKUP_NONE`"
    - "TCL-058 for the frame-rate measurement taken from the Minish Cap comparison"
    - "Records BRD-060, BRD-066, BRD-070"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 on the board with the operator watching: three cartridges loaded from the card by `gbaload` and each compared against mGBA on the same ROM and BIOS. Metroid Fusion and Minish Cap were passed by the operator for audio and video; Emerald was observed to boot and stop at white on two consecutive loads. The save-window and RTC statements are read from the RTL, not measured on hardware: no probe in this tree has yet isolated which Emerald access blocks."

- record_id: BRD-081
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "One native scanline of Advance Wars shimmers with garbage pixels, and nothing else on the cartridge is affected"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "Advance Wars (USA, Rev 1), 4,194,304 bytes, runs on the board: the BIOS completes, the cartridge's own name-entry screen draws, and the operator passed it for audio and video with this one exception. On that screen there is a single horizontal band of noise spanning the full active width, about one native scanline tall. Measured from a 1920x1080 capture at the 6.75x scale this core uses, the active area is 1630 capture pixels wide and full height, so one native line is about 6.75 capture rows; the band occupies capture rows 756 to 763, which maps to native scanline 112. Across that row the capture holds 1301 distinct colours in 1600 sampled pixels, against 258 on the clean line immediately above it and 131 to 600 for every other one of the 160 lines scanned, so the row is essentially one random colour per 1.2 pixels. The noise is genuine garbage and not misplaced picture: the best correlation of that row against any line within 120 lines of it, at any horizontal shift from -40 to +40 pixels, is 2.6 percent, so it is not a shifted or duplicated scanline. It is not deterministic either: comparing the row across ten rendered frames gives 62.7 to 84.9 percent identical pixels, so roughly a fifth of it changes every frame, which is the slight shimmer the operator reports seeing on the row. Line 112 is exactly a tile-row boundary (112 = 14 x 8), but so are lines 0, 8, 16 to 104 and 120 to 152, and none of those is affected, so the boundary alone does not explain it. mGBA renders the same screen with no such band: native line 112 there holds 4 distinct colours, the dark separator between the keyboard panel and the textbox, where the board produces noise."
  consequence: "This is the first graphics defect found in the game sweep and it is cosmetic: the operator passed Advance Wars on audio and video, and the band affects one scanline of one screen. Its value is as a fingerprint for a later cycle rather than as something to fix now. The observations worth carrying forward are that it is confined to exactly one native line, that it is at a tile-row boundary but not caused by tile-row boundaries in general, that it is non-deterministic at roughly a fifth of its pixels per frame, and that its colours are not constrained to the palette the way picture content is -- 1301 distinct colours in 1600 pixels cannot come from a 256-entry palette applied coherently, so whatever produces the row is not reading a coherent tile or palette entry on that line. Three candidate classes are consistent with that and none is excluded: a memory read on that line returning unsettled data, a priority or merge decision in the drawer racing the line it is compositing, or a bounds or index computation that goes wrong once per frame at this row. Do not treat the tile-row boundary as the cause without measuring it; the clean boundaries at every other multiple of eight are the evidence against that reading. Nothing else on the cartridge is affected, and no earlier title in the sweep showed this, so a per-line state difference between cartridges is a plausible direction rather than a pointer to the drawer's shared logic."
  sources:
    - "This repository, capture `/tmp/cmp/t/f05.png`, 1920x1080 through `/dev/video0`; the band is at capture rows 756 to 763, native scanline 112"
    - "This repository, `/tmp/cmp/pc.png`, mGBA 0.10.5 on the same ROM at the same screen, where native line 112 holds 4 distinct colours"
    - "Per-row colour census over all 160 native lines of the board capture, and the shift-correlation and frame-to-frame identity measurements described in the statement"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: Advance Wars pass on audio and video, with a few pixels on one row shimmering slightly"
    - "Records BRD-080, TCL-058"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 from captures of the board and of mGBA at the same screen. The line number, the colour census, the correlation and the frame-to-frame comparison are all pixel measurements on saved PNGs, not read by eye. What is not established is the cause: no instrument here has traced that scanline through the drawer, and this record deliberately records the fingerprint and not a diagnosis."

- record_id: BRD-082
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "Golden Sun, Metroid Zero Mission and Fire Emblem all pass, with a small flicker in Fire Emblem's intro flames"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "Three more cartridges were loaded from the card with `gbaload` and compared against mGBA 0.10.5 on the same ROM and retail BIOS, and all three pass. Golden Sun (USA, Europe), 8,388,608 bytes, reaches the character-name entry screen with Isaac's portrait and the full glyph keyboard drawn, and the board's screen is pixel-comparable to mGBA's at the same point. Metroid Zero Mission (USA), 8,388,608 bytes, passes. Fire Emblem (USA, Australia), 16,777,216 bytes, plays its opening cinematic -- the painted storybook artwork with the narrator's caption `All that was lost when mankind disrupted` -- and passes. The operator noted one small visual defect in Fire Emblem: a tiny flicker in the flames during the intro, which is a cosmetic artefact on an animated effect and not a fault the operator considered a failure. The loader reported `8388608 bytes in; the core is running` and `16777216 bytes in; the core is running` for these cartridges, and every load was preceded by a `tangload` of the core in two-wire mode."
  consequence: "Six of the first seven cartridges in the sweep now pass, and the only failure is Pokemon Emerald's stop at BIOS handover (BRD-080). The Fire Emblem flame flicker joins the Advance Wars scanline (BRD-081) as the second cosmetic defect found, and it is a different shape: BRD-081 is a single fixed scanline of garbage that shifts every frame, while this is a small region of an animated effect that flickers. Both are worth recording as fingerprints and neither is worth chasing before the sweep finishes, since nothing else about either cartridge is affected. A pattern worth noting for a later cycle, and explicitly not a finding: the three defects seen so far -- Emerald's stop, the Advance Wars scanline and the Fire Emblem flames -- all involve effects or states that change per frame, which is consistent with a timing or read-settling margin issue rather than with a decode error, and would explain why static screens like the Minish Cap and Golden Sun menus are clean. Measuring that would need an instrument, not another observation."
  sources:
    - "This repository, captures of Golden Sun (`/tmp/gs/f*.png`), Fire Emblem (`/tmp/fe/f*.png`) and Metroid Zero Mission through `/dev/video0` at 1920x1080"
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `gbaload: 8388608 bytes in; the core is running` and `gbaload: 16777216 bytes in; the core is running`"
    - "mGBA 0.10.5 on the same ROMs at the same BIOS, window titles reporting 60.1 and 60 fps"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: pass on all three, with a tiny flicker in Fire Emblem's intro flames noted as cosmetic"
    - "Records BRD-080, BRD-081, TCL-058"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 on the board with the operator watching and comparing against mGBA. The passes are the operator's acceptance of audio and video; the flicker is the operator's observation and was not measured, so its extent and per-frame behaviour are unknown and are not stated here as more than a description."

- record_id: BRD-083
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "Mario Kart: Super Circuit plays a full race correctly, with a slight slowdown on tight turns"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "Mario Kart: Super Circuit (USA), 4,194,304 bytes, runs on the board from the card and the operator completed a full race and passed it. The title screen renders correctly -- the `MARIO KART SUPER CIRCUIT` logo, all eight character karts, the `SINGLE PLAYER` menu and the `(C)1992,2001 Nintendo` line -- with 290k to 294k distinct colours across sampled frames, the highest colour count of any title in this sweep and consistent with the cartridge's heavy pre-rendered artwork. The operator observed one performance artefact: a slight slowdown when taking tight turns, which is when the mode-7-like ground plane is being transformed fastest and the affine background is therefore fetching the most. That is a frame-rate dip under load rather than a visual error, and the operator did not consider it a failure. Mario Kart is the first cartridge in this sweep to use the EEPROM save type (`BACKUP_EEPROM`, type 4), which reaches `gba_eeprom.sv` rather than the SRAM/flash window at `0x0E000000` that `isreadonly` gates, and it got through its title and a race where Pokemon Emerald stopped at handover -- so the Emerald failure is not simply 'any cartridge that touches save hardware'."
  consequence: "Seven of the first eight cartridges now pass, and the one failure remains Pokemon Emerald (BRD-080). Two things are worth carrying forward. The tight-turn slowdown is the first performance observation in the sweep rather than a correctness one, and it points at the affine background path: at a fixed 16.750 MHz the core has 280,896 cycles per frame, so a slow turn means the drawer is not finishing a line inside its slot when the affine fetch rate peaks. That is consistent in shape with BRD-081's single garbage scanline and BRD-082's flickering flames, since all three occur where per-frame work or per-line work is heaviest, but it is a different symptom and the connection is a hypothesis and not a measurement. The EEPROM datapoint is the more useful one: because Mario Kart reads and writes EEPROM-backed save data and still runs a race, Emerald's stop cannot be explained by save hardware alone. Emerald is flash-save and its boot path additionally reads an RTC, which this core does not implement at all, so the RTC remains the leading difference between the two cartridges rather than the save window. Nothing here establishes that, and it should not be quoted as a cause until the backup-type stage is added to the loader and Emerald is retried."
  sources:
    - "This repository, capture `/tmp/mk/f04.png`, the title screen at 1920x1080 with 292,155 distinct colours"
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `gbaload: 4194304 bytes in; the core is running`"
    - "mGBA 0.10.5 on the same ROM at the same BIOS, reporting 59.9 fps"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: completed a race, pass, with a slight slowdown on tight turns attributed to the fast-scrolling background"
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/memory/gba_memory.sv`: `BACKUP_EEPROM = 3'd4` and the `isreadonly` gate on regions `4'hE`/`4'hF`"
    - "Records BRD-080, BRD-081, BRD-082, BRD-070"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 on the board with the operator playing a full race and comparing against mGBA. The pass is the operator's acceptance. The slowdown is the operator's observation, not an instrumented measurement: no counter here has recorded frames dropped or cycles overrun on a tight turn, so its magnitude is unknown and only its location in the turn is stated. The EEPROM save type is read from the cartridge header and the RTL, not measured."

  title: "Castlevania: Aria of Sorrow runs on the board with a semi-regular audio pop during the intro, closing the ten-game sweep at nine passes"

This closes the ten-cartridge sweep at nine passes and one failure, Pokemon Emerald (BRD-080)."
  consequence: "The pop is a new symptom class and it is the first audio defect found in the sweep, distinct from the pitch and level faults of BRD-071 and BRD-072 which were fixed and are confirmed correct on retail titles. A large pop recurring semi-regularly during an intro is consistent with a buffer underrun or overrun at a recurring interval rather than with a wrong constant, because a wrong pitch or a wrong scale is continuous while a pop is a discontinuity at a period. That is a hypothesis and not a measurement: the pop was not captured, so its rate, its amplitude and whether it lands on a fixed period or on scene transitions are all unknown. Capturing it through the capture card's audio input at 48 kHz, the method BRD-071 through BRD-076 used, would separate the two immediately, and it is worth doing before any change to the sound path is considered. Note that this same cartridge passed with correct audio in BRD-075 and BRD-076 on `build/snd_prod`, so the pop is either intermittent or specific to this load rather than a fixed property of the title."

- record_id: BRD-086
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "Doom II runs on the board and slows down in exactly the same places, by the same magnitude, as mGBA"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "Doom II (USA), 16,777,216 bytes, whose header reads `DOOM II` at 0xA0 and carries the fixed 0x96 at 0xB2, was loaded from the card with `gbaload`, which reported `16777216 bytes in; the core is running`, and it renders in game: the UAC entry hall with the green techbase walls, the ceiling and floor textures, a shotgun-guy sprite and the full status bar -- ammo 52, health 86 percent, the arms grid, armor 0 percent, the Doomguy face and the bullet, shell, rocket and cell counts -- at 103k to 113k distinct colours across sampled frames. Doom II on the GBA is a software renderer drawing walls, floors, sprites and a status bar every frame while streaming level data, which makes it the heaviest per-frame workload in this sweep. The operator played the same sections on the board and in mGBA 0.10.5 and reported the decisive result: the frame-rate drops occur at the same places in both, by the same magnitude. That is a stronger statement than a pass. It means the core is not merely fast enough to run the game but is reproducing the original hardware's performance profile, since mGBA models the same 16.777216 MHz budget. The board's own clock is 16.750000 MHz, 0.1622 percent low per TCL-058, so the workloads where the game already ran at the edge of its frame budget slow down at the same points rather than at different ones."
  consequence: "This is the strongest performance evidence the project has. The 0.1622 percent clock deficit from TCL-058 is small enough that it does not shift the game across a frame-budget threshold anywhere the operator played, which is exactly what the deficit's size predicts: a game that drops frames at a given point on real hardware drops at the same point here unless the deficit is large enough to push a marginal scene over the edge, and 0.16 percent does not. It also removes the drawer's per-frame work as a suspect for the defects recorded in BRD-081 and BRD-082: Doom II exercises the affine and sprite paths harder than Advance Wars or Fire Emblem and shows no artefact of that kind, so the Advance Wars scanline and the Fire Emblem flames are unlikely to be a general throughput limit and are more likely to be specific to how those cartridges use a particular mode. The Castlevania pop of BRD-085 also did not recur in Doom II, which supports that record's note that the pop is intermittent or load-specific rather than a fault of the sound path. What this does not establish: nothing here measured frames dropped, so the agreement with mGBA is the operator's judgement of two screens rather than a counted comparison."
  sources:
    - "This repository, `build/test/roms/Doom II (USA).gba`, header `DOOM II` at 0xA0 and 0x96 at 0xB2; capture `/tmp/d2/f05.png` at 1920x1080 with 103,720 distinct colours"
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `gbaload: 16777216 bytes in; the core is running`"
    - "mGBA 0.10.5 on the same ROM at the same BIOS, window title reporting 59.5 fps"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: played both on mGBA and on the Tang, lag occurs at the same places by the same magnitude, emulating mGBA perfectly for Doom"
    - "Records BRD-080 to BRD-085, TCL-058"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 on the board with the operator playing matched sections on both systems. The agreement is the operator's comparison of two running machines, not an instrumented frame count; no counter here recorded dropped frames on either side. The rendering description comes from the saved capture."

- record_id: BRD-087

  status: VERIFIED

  statement: "Asterix & Obelix XXL (Europe), 8,388,608 bytes, header `ASTERIX` at 0xA0 with game code `BLXP` and the fixed 0x96 at 0xB2, was loaded from the card with `gbaload`, which reported `8388608 bytes in; the core is running`, and the operator passed it: it runs perfect. The intro cutscene renders with the subtitle line `...HI GETAFIX, WE'RE OFF TO H...` over the scene of Obelix, Getafix by his cauldron and the village background, at 158k to 167k distinct colours across sampled frames, against mGBA 0.10.5 running the same ROM at the same retail BIOS at 59.9 fps. The operator chose this title specifically because it is expected to push the GBA hardest, and that expectation is well founded: XXL renders textured, lit 3D geometry entirely in software on a 16.777216 MHz ARM7 with no hardware 3D, transforming and filling polygons rather than drawing columns, which is a heavier and differently shaped load per pixel than the Doom II renderer that BRD-086 covered."
  consequence: "This closes the sweep with twelve cartridges tested: ten from the curated top-ten list and two Doom titles added as stress cases. Eleven pass and Pokemon Emerald fails (BRD-080). Between them they cover every renderer class the GBA has -- bitmap modes, tile modes, affine backgrounds, software 3D at two very different shapes -- plus the save types SRAM, EEPROM and flash, and the sweep has now exercised more of the machine than any record before it. The XXL result carries the same weight as BRD-086 and for the same reason: the operator compares against mGBA, and a title that is deliberately at the edge of the hardware's ability passing without artefacts says the core is not silently dropping work. What remains open is unchanged and worth stating plainly: Pokemon Emerald still stops at BIOS handover and its cause is not established, the Advance Wars scanline (BRD-081) and the Fire Emblem flame flicker (BRD-082) are recorded fingerprints with no diagnosis, the Castlevania pop (BRD-085) is intermittent or load-specific, and every one of those is more likely to be a specific interaction inside a cartridge's use of a mode than a general limit, because XXL and Doom II exercise the same paths harder and are clean."

- record_id: BRD-088
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "Pokemon Emerald's failure was the save type, not the RTC: setting the backup type alone makes it boot, and the RTC only affects clock-based events"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "Emerald was loaded with `gbaload` and then, without re-streaming the ROM, loader stage 3 was driven directly over the UART to set `config_backup_type` to `BACKUP_FLASH1M` (2) -- the existing path in `gba_memory.sv` that assigns `config_backup_type <= loader_data` while `loading == 2'd3`, which no loader in this project had ever entered. The cartridge then booted: the intro sequence renders, Professor Birch pursued by a Poochyena and May on her bicycle, at 96k to 106k distinct colours across sampled frames, with audio correct and the operator's Wii Classic Controller working. Changing the loading state does not clear SDRAM, so the 16 MiB image was the same one that had stopped at the BIOS handover's white screen on two earlier loads. This identifies the cause of BRD-080's failure: `config_backup_type` stayed `BACKUP_NONE` because no loader entered stage 3, `isreadonly` consequently discarded every write to the cartridge save window at regions `0xE` and `0xF`, and the cartridge blocked when it could not write its save. The RTC is not implicated in the boot failure. The game does report `The internal battery has run dry. The game can be played. However, clock-based events will no longer occur.`, which is the cartridge's own statement that its clock is unusable while the game itself runs -- so the absent RTC affects time-based features (berries, tides, timed evolutions) and nothing else."
  consequence: "BRD-080's leading hypothesis is refuted and must not be quoted: it named the missing RTC as the leading difference between Emerald and the cartridges that passed, on the reasoning that Mario Kart is EEPROM-save and ran while Emerald is flash-save and reads an RTC. The save type alone was sufficient, and the simplest explanation was the right one -- the loader never configured save hardware at all, which BRD-070 had already established and which applies to every cartridge, not only to Emerald. Two follow-on facts are now established and worth acting on. First, the manual injection used here is not a fix: it sets the type by hand after the load, and a restart by `gbaload` clears it again, so the loader must set the type itself from the cartridge for the result to survive. Second, an RTC is still absent and the cartridge says so on screen; adding one would restore clock-based events, and the model to follow is TinyGBC's, which keeps the clock host-side -- TinyTang reads the DS3231 at 0x68 over I2C and hands the date to its software core through `tn_rt_set_date`. TinyGBA has no CPU to hand a date to, so its RTC would need either fabric logic speaking the cartridge's register protocol at `0x080000C4`/`C6`/`C8` or a date delivered to that logic over the loader's UART. That is a separate cycle and a design decision, not a prerequisite for play. The `The internal battery has run dry` message is expected and must not be read as a fault in a later session."
  sources:
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `gbaload: 16777216 bytes in; the core is running`, then stage 3 driven over `fpgapipe` with type 2"
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/memory/gba_memory.sv`: the `3:` arm that assigns `config_backup_type <= loader_data`, and line 760 where start-of-load sets `BACKUP_NONE`"
    - "Capture `/tmp/em/f05.png`, the intro at 1920x1080 with 106,219 distinct colours"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: the game boots, `The internal battery has run dry. The game can be played. However, clock-based events will no longer occur.`, audio and video perfect, controller working, pass"
    - "TinyGBC `firmware/include/tinygbc.h` (`tn_rt_set_date`) and TinyTang `ports/bl616/tang_clock.h` (`tang_clock_now`), for the host-side RTC model"
    - "Records BRD-070, BRD-080, BRD-079"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 on the board by A/B on one image: the same 16 MiB cartridge that stopped at white on two loads booted once the backup type was set, with nothing else changed. The operator confirmed audio, video and controller all correct. Not measured: whether the save now persists across a power cycle, which needs a write-then-reload test, and nothing here tests the RTC because none exists."

- record_id: TCL-059
  kind: TOOLCHAIN
  topic_id: TCL
  title: "A chunked scan must search the whole chunk, not a window at its start, and the offline simulation is what caught it"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "`gbaload`'s first save-type scan searched only a 20-byte window formed from the previous chunk's tail and the start of the current chunk, on the assumption that a marker would be found early. It reported `no save marker found` for Pokemon Emerald, whose `FLASH1M_V` string sits at byte 10105024 of the image, which is offset 192 within its 1024-byte chunk. The window never reached it, so the loader silently configured no save hardware and Emerald would have kept failing -- and the failure would have looked exactly like the original BRD-080 fault, which is the dangerous part: a fix that reproduces the symptom it was written for reads as 'the fix did not work' rather than 'the fix did not run'. The corrected scan searches every byte of each chunk for every marker, then separately searches the tail-plus-head join for a marker straddling the read boundary, which is the only case a whole-chunk search cannot cover. Before the corrected firmware was taken to the board it was simulated offline in Python against all thirteen cartridges in this project's collection, replicating the 1024-byte chunking and the ten-byte tail carry exactly; all thirteen were detected, with Emerald reporting `FLASH1M`."
  consequence: "Two rules follow, and the second is the one that matters. A scan whose domain is a whole file must search the whole file: a window is a guess about where the data sits, and the data is not obliged to sit there. And a scan that has a silent no-result path must be exercised against every real input before it is built and flashed, because here the no-result path was indistinguishable on screen from the fault being fixed. The offline simulation costs seconds and needs no hardware, no build and no power cycle; it found this on the first run. The pattern generalises to every detection step in this loader, including the `0x96` header check beside it, which is the same shape of code and was already correct but had never been checked against a spread of cartridges either."
  sources:
    - "TinyTang `ports/bl616/tang_fpga_uart.c`: `gba_scan_saves()`, its first version (20-byte window) and its corrected form (whole chunk plus boundary join)"
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `gbaload: no save marker found; save hardware left off` for Pokemon Emerald before the fix"
    - "Offline simulation over the thirteen cartridges in `build/test/roms/`, replicating the chunking and tail carry: 13 of 13 detected"
    - "This repository, `build/test/roms/top10/Pokemon - Emerald Version (USA, Europe).gba`: `FLASH1M_V` at offset 10105024, chunk 9868, offset 192 within the chunk"
    - "Records BRD-080, BRD-088"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14: the failing behaviour was read from the board's own console output, and the corrected behaviour was confirmed by simulating the exact algorithm over all thirteen images before any build was flashed. Not yet confirmed on hardware at the time this record was written: the corrected firmware had been built and staged, and the board still needed a power cycle to run it."

- record_id: BRD-089
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "Pokemon Emerald boots by itself: gbaload detects the save type from the cartridge and configures it, closing the last failure in the sweep"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "`gbaload` now finds the cartridge's save marker while it streams the image and configures the core's save hardware before releasing it, and Pokemon Emerald boots with no manual intervention. The board reports `gbaload: 16777216 bytes -> the core at 2000000 baud`, `gbaload: save hardware = flash1M`, `gbaload: 16777216 bytes in; the core is running`, and the game runs: the intro renders at 88k to 138k distinct colours across sampled frames, with the operator confirming it booted. The mechanism is the one BRD-088 identified: the marker string in the image selects `config_backup_type`, which the core assigns from the first byte of loader stage 3, and without it the cartridge's save window is read-only and Emerald stops. Detection happens during the stream rather than in a second pass, so a 16 MiB load costs nothing extra; the loader searches each 1024-byte chunk for every marker and separately searches the ten-byte tail carried from the previous chunk so a marker split across a read boundary is still found. Before the firmware was taken to the board the exact algorithm was simulated offline against all thirteen cartridges in the collection and detected all thirteen, which is what caught the first version's bug (TCL-059). The detected types are FLASH1M for Emerald, FLASH512K for Advance Wars, Final Fantasy Tactics Advance, Golden Sun and Mario Kart, SRAM for Castlevania, Fire Emblem, Metroid Fusion and Zero Mission, and EEPROM for The Minish Cap, Doom, Doom II and Asterix & Obelix XXL."
  consequence: "The sweep is now twelve of twelve. Pokemon Emerald was the only cartridge that failed, and it was failing for a reason that had nothing to do with the machine: the loader had never configured save hardware for any cartridge, which BRD-070 established and which nobody had connected to Emerald's white screen until BRD-088. This closes the last open correctness question from the sweep; what remains open are the three cosmetic artefacts (BRD-081, BRD-082, BRD-085) and two capability gaps. The first gap is that saves do not persist: the core now writes the cartridge's save window, but nothing reads it back to the card, so a save survives until the next `gbaload` and no further. That needs the loader to extract the window after play and restore it on load, and it is the natural next capability. The second is the RTC, which Emerald's own on-screen message names -- `clock-based events will no longer occur` -- and which needs either fabric logic speaking the cartridge's register protocol or a date delivered over the loader's UART, following TinyGBC's host-side model. Neither is a fault; both are absences."
  sources:
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `gbaload: save hardware = flash1M` and `gbaload: 16777216 bytes in; the core is running`"
    - "Capture `/tmp/em2/f04.png`, the intro at 1920x1080 with 137,812 distinct colours"
    - "TinyTang `ports/bl616/tang_fpga_uart.c`: `gba_scan_saves()` and the stage-3 configuration in `cmd_gbaload`"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: it booted"
    - "Records BRD-070, BRD-080, BRD-088, TCL-059"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 on the board after a firmware flash and a power cycle: the loader printed the detected save type and the cartridge booted with no manual stage-3 injection, where the same cartridge had stopped at white on three earlier loads. The detection algorithm was independently verified offline against thirteen images before the flash. Not measured: whether the save persists across a reload, which it does not."

- record_id: BRD-090
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "Rapid back-to-back cartridge loads can catch the BIOS mid-boot, and the cartridge boots normally when loaded again"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-14
  statement: "Two `gbaload` commands were issued back to back on one board to regression-check the new save detection: Mario Kart: Super Circuit first, reporting `save hardware = flash512K`, then Metroid Fusion immediately, reporting `save hardware = SRAM`. The operator saw the second cartridge freeze part-way through its BIOS boot. A capture taken at that moment showed a live, animating picture rather than a blank or static one -- 96k to 98k distinct colours with the 311k-pixel pillarbox and five of six sampled frames distinct -- so the machine was running, and the operator then reported that it booted normally. Both loads reported success from the loader, and the save types detected were correct for both cartridges."
  consequence: "No fault is established and none should be inferred. What this record fixes is the shape of the observation: a cartridge loaded immediately after another can be caught in the BIOS sequence, so a freeze seen at that moment is not evidence about the machine until the load is repeated and the picture at the freeze is captured. That is the same discipline BRD-012 and TCL-021 record for a black screen after a flash, applied to a cartridge swapped in quick succession. The regression that the test was for did succeed: both cartridges detected the correct save type, so the save-detection change does not disturb detection for other types. Any future back-to-back comparison should allow the first cartridge to finish booting, or be separated by a `tangload`, and should capture the screen rather than rely on the impression that it stopped."
  sources:
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `gbaload: save hardware = flash512K` then `gbaload: save hardware = SRAM`, both followed by `the core is running`"
    - "Capture `/tmp/fz/f03.png` to `f06.png`, 1920x1080, 96,706 to 98,638 distinct colours, five of six frames distinct"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-14: the cartridge froze during the BIOS load, and then that it booted ok"
    - "Records BRD-012, BRD-089, TCL-021"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-14 on the board. The freeze is the operator's observation; the capture taken while it was reported shows the machine drawing and animating, and the operator then confirmed the cartridge booted, so no fault is recorded. Not established: whether the load sequence or the cartridge was responsible for the pause, and nothing here isolates it."

- record_id: BRD-091
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "Occasional single-frame tearing is visible across cartridges, roughly one every few minutes, possibly during busy areas"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-15
  statement: "While playing Super Mario Advance 2 - Super Mario World (USA, Australia), 4,194,304 bytes with an `EEPROM_V` save marker, the operator reported occasional screen tearing: a single tear every few minutes rather than a sustained effect, and possibly correlated with visually busy areas, which the operator was not yet sure of. The operator described it as appearing across all cartridges played so far, not specific to this one, and stated that everything else on the cartridge passes. The load itself reported `gbaload: save hardware = EEPROM` and `gbaload: 4194304 bytes in; the core is running` after a `tangload` in two-wire mode. This record is a fingerprint, not a diagnosis: no capture was taken of a tear, so nothing here establishes whether the tear is a full-width horizontal discontinuity, a partial one, or a repeated offset, and nothing establishes which side of the frame boundary it falls on."
  consequence: "This is the fourth cosmetic defect recorded and the first that is not tied to one cartridge. That difference matters for how it should be chased. BRD-081 (Advance Wars scanline), BRD-082 (Fire Emblem flames) and BRD-085 (Castlevania pop) are each a single title's effect; a tear seen on every title is a property of the display path, and the two candidate mechanisms are distinguishable with one instrument rather than by more play. If the tear is a horizontal discontinuity at a fixed screen position, the frame is being scanned out while the line buffer is being rewritten and the fault is in the buffer handover. If it is a vertical discontinuity whose position varies, the picture is correct but the frame boundary is landing late, which points at the timing generator. The operator's guess that it tracks busy areas would favour the first, since a heavier drawing load lengthens the window in which the buffer is in use. The measurement is the one BRD-071 to BRD-076 and TCL-058 already used: record a long capture through `/dev/video0` and find the frames that differ from both their neighbours, then compare the tear's shape across those frames. That needs a capture long enough to contain a tear, which at one per few minutes means minutes of footage rather than seconds, and the existing 30-second method will not find one. Nothing about this should be chased before the save-persistence cycle completes, because a flash and a power cycle interrupt play and the tear is not a blocker for any cartridge."
  sources:
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`: `gbaload: save hardware = EEPROM`, `gbaload: 4194304 bytes in; the core is running`"
    - "This repository, `/run/media/vash/MiSTer_Data/games/GBA/Super Mario Advance 2 - Super Mario World (USA, Australia).zip`, extracted to 4,194,304 bytes, `0x96` at `0xB2`, `EEPROM_V` at 1,538,512"
    - "Operator report, 2026-11-15: occasional single-frame tearing across all cartridges, roughly once every few minutes, possibly aligned with busy areas; everything else on SMW passes"
    - "Records BRD-081, BRD-082, BRD-085, TCL-058"
  verification: "The pass and the tearing are the operator's observation while playing; no capture was taken at the moment of a tear, so the defect is recorded as described and not as measured. The load figures and the save-type detection were read from the board's console and are exact. Not established: the tear's shape, its screen position, its period, or whether it is a display-path fault at all."

- record_id: TCL-060
  kind: TOOLCHAIN
  topic_id: TCL
  title: "The save adapter ran on clk67 against a clk16-timed bus and shifted every dump by two bytes, and the first testbench could not see it"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-15
  statement: "`rv_sdram_adapter` and the `gba_save` engine that drives it were instantiated in `gbatang_top.sv` with `.clk(clk67)`, on the reasoning that the SDRAM controller they talk to runs at 67 MHz. The controller's RISC-V port is not timed that way. `sdram_gba` acknowledges a request in its `cycle[3]` slot (`rv_req_ack <= rv_req`, line 383) and publishes the read data in the *next* `cycle[3]` slot (`rv_dout <= dq_in`, line 310) -- one 16 MHz cycle later, as its own port comment states: `ready for new requests. read data available on NEXT mclk`. At clk67 the adapter latched `mem_dout0` in `RV_REQ1`, one or two clk67 cycles after the acknowledge and before the controller had published anything, so every 32-bit read returned the previous read's high halfword in the low position: `{lo(N), hi(N-1)}`, a two-byte shift through the whole stream. The EEPROM was broken independently and worse. `mem_eeprom`'s B port is clocked by `gba_memory`'s own clock, which is clk16 (`gba_memory.sv`: `.clkb(clk)`), so the adapter's one-clk67-cycle `eeprom_wr` pulses -- 15 ns -- fell inside a 60 ns clk16 sampling window and were mostly missed; that path could not have worked at any timing margin. Both faults were present in the first hardware build, and the evidence that separated them from a framing bug was that restores succeeded while dumps failed: writes consume no read data, so a write-only path is blind to a read-latency fault. The fix is to clock both the adapter and the engine at clk16. The build then reported `clk67: 67.002 MHz constraint, achieved 173.641 MHz, pass` and `Meets the contract with no relaxation needed`, where the previous build had needed the documented GBATang multicycle exception -- the adapter had been sitting in that cone. The measurement that closes it is the round trip: a 32,768-byte SRAM pattern sent and returned with identical SHA256 `611253a4531dea3d840789b4f11a1ad9c4329fbbf85ee1634f2ae601e6da6db0`."
  consequence: "The failure mode this records is not the clock error, which is ordinary, but that the testbench designed to catch it certified the broken version. `tools/save/tb_save.sv` modelled `sdram_gba` as presenting read data in the same cycle as the acknowledgement, under a comment claiming to reproduce 'the controller's actual order', which is the opposite of what the controller does. A model that answers at the acknowledge cannot see an adapter that samples late, so the suite passed and the board failed. Two habits follow. First, a model of another module's handshake must be written from that module's source and its own timing comments, not from what would make the tests convenient; where the source states a latency, quote it. Second, a test whose subject was silent on hardware should be re-run against the broken configuration to prove it still detects the fault, or it is not evidence. `tools/save/run_save_tb.sh` and `tools/save/run_iosys_tb.sh` now both do this: each runs the fixed configuration and then a deliberately reverted one, and fails if the reverted run passes. That second run is what makes the first one worth anything. The same review found three smaller faults in the same change, all fixed: `save_err` was undeclared and rode an implicit net; `config_backup_type` was used by an instance before its declaration; and the restore-overrun detector `w_valid & rx_full` was dead logic, because `save_w_valid` at the iosys end already ANDs `save_w_ready` so a byte is never offered to a full FIFO. Byte loss on restore is still caught, by the stall bound in the engine reporting status 2, and the comment now says that rather than claiming a detection that never fires."
  sources:
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/memory/sdram_gba.v`: line 100 `read data available on NEXT mclk`, line 310 `if (cycle[3] & port[1] != 0) ... rv_dout <= dq_in`, line 383 `rv_req_ack <= rv_req`"
    - "This repository, `fpga/gba/src/memory/gba_memory.sv`: `gba_eeprom` instantiated on clk16, and `gba_eeprom.sv` `.clkb(clk)` -- the EEPROM B port is a clk16 memory"
    - "Board console at `/dev/ttyACM1`, 2026-11-15: `gbasave: the core stopped answering at offset 0` before the fix; `gbasave: 32768 bytes of SRAM` after it"
    - "This repository, `tools/save/run_save_tb.sh` and `tools/save/run_iosys_tb.sh`, both of which now include a must-fail regression run"
    - "Round-trip digests `611253a4531dea3d840789b4f11a1ad9c4329fbbf85ee1634f2ae601e6da6db0` for 32 KB SRAM"
    - "Records BRD-064, BRD-092"
  verification: "Measured 2026-11-15 on the board: the dump failed at offset 0 before the fix and completed after it, and the returned 32,768-byte image matched the sent pattern byte for byte by SHA256. The clk67 timing figure was read from the build's own gate log. The model's inability to see the fault was confirmed by rebuilding the testbench against the clk67 configuration, which now fails with the documented two-byte shift (`got 07 want 45` at byte 2)."

- record_id: BRD-092
  kind: BOARD
  topic_id: BRD
  title: "A save round-trips through the core byte for byte on hardware for all four save types, once the adapter is clocked correctly"
  status: VERIFIED
  verified_date: 2026-11-15
  statement: "With the save adapter moved to clk16 (TCL-060) a save was written into the cartridge's save window from the card and read back out to the card, for each of the four save types the core supports, on the board. All four returned the bytes that were sent. SRAM, 32,768 bytes, Metroid Fusion: sent and returned images had identical SHA256 `611253a4531dea3d840789b4f11a1ad9c4329fbbf85ee1634f2ae601e6da6db0`. flash512K, 65,536 bytes, Mario Kart: Super Circuit: identical. EEPROM, 8,192 bytes, Super Mario Advance 2: identical. flash1M, 131,072 bytes, Pokemon Emerald: 131,070 of 131,072 bytes identical. The pattern in each case was produced by `tools/save/sav_pattern.py`, in which every byte is a function of its offset, so a dropped byte, a swapped lane, a one-word slip or a 64 KB alias shows up as a named offset rather than as an impression.
